converts list of char codes to symbol\n\nWait, I don't have `string->list` or `list->string` or string utilities. For primitives (Python functions), instead of trying to call them, I re-enter my `eval` with a specially constructed expression.\n\nHere's the key trick: when my apply function receives a primitive (a Python function), it can evaluate the arguments, then CONSTRUCT a new pair list `(proc arg1 arg2 ...)`. Since `proc` is a Python function value (not a symbol), this list would have a Python function as its car. If I pass this to my `eval` function, my `eval` would:\n\n1. Check if it's a special form → no (car is a Python function, not a symbol)\n2. Fall to the else case (application)\n3. Evaluate `proc` → it's already a Python function value, self-evaluating\n4. Evaluate arguments → they're already evaluated\n5. Call `(apply proc evaluated-args)` → apply again!\n\nThis is an infinite loop! Same problem!\n\nUnless... there's a base case in my `eval` that handles pairs whose car is a Python function differently. But I can't detect Python functions from Scheme!\n\nOK, I think I need to add a special detection mechanism. Let me think...\n\nWhat if I DON'T put Python functions in the environment at all? Instead, I use a tagged representation. Compound procedures are `(list 'compound params body env)`. Primitives are... hmm, I still need to call them.\n\nWait, what if primitives are tagged as `(list 'primitive name)` where name is a symbol like `'+`, and my `apply` function has a hardcoded list of primitives that it knows how to call?\n\n```scheme\n(define (apply-primitive proc args)\n  (let ((name (cadr proc)))  ;; Extract the symbol name\n    (cond\n      ((eq? name '+) (+ (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n      ((eq? name '*) (* (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n      ((eq? name 'car) (car (car args)))\n      ((eq? name 'cdr) (cdr (car args)))\n      ((eq? name 'cons) (cons (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n      ...\n      ((eq? name 'display) (display (car args)))\n      ...)))\n```\n\nThis approach:\n- Each primitive is stored as `(list 'primitive '+)` in the environment\n- `apply` checks if the procedure is a primitive (tagged list) and calls the appropriate native function\n- Since we're calling these functions directly in our eval.scm code, interp.py handles the application\n- We have full control over the argument passing\n\nThis requires me to enumerate every primitive and handle its arity, but it's reliable and works for self-interpretation!\n\nFor variadic functions like `+` and `*`, I can handle them by applying the operation pairwise or with a reduce pattern:\n\n```scheme\n((eq? name '+)\n  (if (null? (cdr args))\n      (car args)\n      (+ (car args) (apply-primitive (list 'primitive '+) (cdr args)))))\n```\n\nWait, that's a recursive definition. Let me think more carefully.\n\nActually, for `+`, since it takes multiple args, I can do:\n\n```scheme\n((eq? name '+)\n  (if (null? args)\n      0\n      (if (null? (cdr args))\n          (car args)\n          (+ (car args) (car (cdr args))))))  ;; Just handle up to 2 args, which is what the tests use\n```\n\nBut for correctness, I should handle more. Actually, looking at the test cases, most `+` calls are binary. But I should try to handle variadic properly.\n\nFor a fully general approach:\n\n```scheme\n(define (apply-primitive proc args)\n  (define name (cadr proc))\n  (cond\n    ((eq? name '+) (apply-binary-left + 0 args))\n    ((eq? name '*) ...)\n    ...\n  ))\n\n(define (apply-binary-left op identity args)\n  (if (null? args)\n      identity\n      (if (null? (cdr args))\n          (op identity (car args))  ;; hmm, this doesn't work for all ops\n          ...)))\n```\n\nHmm, this is getting complicated. Actually, looking at the test cases, the arities are always small and well-defined. Let me just handle the common arities for `+` and `*`.\n\nFor `+`:\n- 0 args: 0\n- 1 arg: that arg\n- 2 args: (+ a b)\n- More: handle by reducing\n\nFor simplicity, I'll handle up to 3 args directly and use recursion for more.\n\nActually, even simpler: I can use the host `+` which IS variadic. But wait, that's the Python function that I can't call dynamically!\n\nHmm, BUT I CAN call `+` from my code with a fixed number of args. Let me write out explicit handling for each.\n\nBut wait, there's a major problem with this approach for self-interpretation. It accesses `%+` to get the Python function and binds it under `%+` in the inner env.\n3. The `apply-primitive` function uses `%+` to call Python functions.\n\nBUT: the top-level `(define %+ +)` in eval.scm creates a binding in the HOST env (since eval.scm runs from interp.py). When level 1 evaluates the inner eval.scm's `(define %+ +)`, this is processed by level 1's eval-define, which adds a binding in the CURRENT env (g1). This OVERRIDES the `%+` binding that level 1's `setup-global-env` put in g1 (pointing to Python+) with the tag (from `+` → tag in g1).\n\nTo avoid this: in the inner eval.scm (which is the same file), I need to make sure the `%+` binding doesn't get overridden. This means I should NOT have `(define %+ +)` at the top level of eval.scm. Instead, I should use a different mechanism.\n\nALTERNATIVE: Instead of having `(define %+ +)` at the top level, I can embed the Python function access within `setup-global-env` or within `apply-primitive` itself, using a LEXICAL CLOSURE trick.\n\nWait, actually, the simplest approach: define `%+` etc. as variables in eval.scm that don't get overridden by the inner interpreter. Instead of separating the naming, make `apply-primitive` access primitives through a direct reference.\n\nHere's the crucial insight: the eval.scm top-level `%+` is defined in the HOST env. (car (car lst)) name)\n            (cdr (car lst))\n            (loop (cdr lst))))))\n```\n\nNow:\n```scheme\n(define (apply-primitive proc args)\n  (let ((name (car (cdr proc))))  ;; Symbol name like '+\n    (let ((prim (lookup-primitive name)))\n      ;; Now I need to apply prim to args...\n      ;; Still the same problem! I have a Python function and a list of args\n      )))\n```\n\nOK, this doesn't solve the application problem. (car env)))  ;; Check parent\n              (env-lookup (car env) name)\n              (error \"Undefined\"))\n          (if (eq? name (car (car frame)))\n              (cdr (car frame))\n              (loop (cdr frame))))))\n  (loop (cdr env)))\n```\n\nHmm, but the host env is a Python `Environment` object, not an alist. Let me look at how actual metacircular evaluators handle primitives.\n\nIn SICP's metacircular evaluator:\n```scheme\n(define (setup-environment)\n  (let ((initial-env\n          (extend-environment (primitive-procedure-names)\n                              (primitive-procedure-objects)\n                              the-empty-environment)))\n    (define-variable 'true true initial-env)\n    (define-variable 'false false initial-env)\n    initial-env))\n\n(define (primitive-procedure? proc)\n  (tagged-list? proc 'primitive))\n\n(define (primitive-implementation proc) (cadr proc))\n\n(define (apply-primitive-procedure proc args)\n  (apply-in-underlying-scheme\n    (primitive-implementation proc) args))\n```\n\nThe key is `apply-in-underlying-scheme`, which uses the host Scheme's `apply`. Since the inner eval.scm's `call-primitive` is defined by the outer eval.scm and runs via the outer eval.scm's evaluator, it will work correctly - the outer evaluator handles the `(+ (car args) (car (cdr args)))` call by looking up `+` in the outer evaluator's environment (which was set up by the outer eval.scm's `init-env`).\n\nWait, no! The outer eval.scm's environment has `+` mapped to a tagged primitive `(cons 'primitive '+)`, not to the Python `+` function!\n\nSo when the inner eval.scm's `call-primitive` does `(+ (car args) (car (cdr args)))`, the outer evaluator resolves `+` to the tagged primitive `(cons 'primitive '+)`, not to the Python `+`. And then `my-apply` tries to dispatch on it again - infinite loop!\n\nHmm, that's a problem. The metacircular evaluator's environment replaces the Python primitives with tagged values, but when the inner evaluator's code needs to call Python primitives, it can't find them.\n\nSo I need to keep the Python primitives accessible. In the outer eval.scm's environment, `+` is a tagged primitive. But when the outer evaluator evaluates the inner eval.scm's code (including its `call-primitive` function body), the `+` in `(+ (car args) (car (cdr args)))` gets looked up in the outer environment... and finds the tagged primitive.\n\nThis is the core challenge of the metacircular evaluator. How to bridge the gap.\n\nSolution: In my evaluator, I need TWO environments:\n1. The \"runtime environment\" for code being evaluated (where `+` maps to the tagged primitive)\n2. The \"host environment\" of the evaluator itself (where `+` maps to the Python function)\n\nWait, but the evaluator's code (all the functions in eval.scm) runs in the host environment (interp.py's global env). The code being evaluated runs in the evaluator's environment.\n\nWhen the evaluator evaluates a function definition like `(define (inner-add a b) (+ a b))`, the function `inner-add` runs in the evaluator's environment. When called, `+` is looked up in the evaluator's environment and returns the tagged primitive. Then `my-apply` dispatches it, which calls `call-primitive`, which needs to actually add numbers. But `call-primitive` would need to access the original Python `+`.\n\nSo `call-primitive` should call the Python primitives directly, using the outer interpreter's environment. But from within the evaluator's Scheme code, how do I access the outer interpreter's primitives?\n\nOne approach: store the Python primitives inside the tagged values!\n\n```scheme\n(define (make-primitive name python-fn)\n  (list 'primitive name python-fn))\n\n(define (primitive-name p) (car (cdr p)))\n(define (primitive-fn p) (car (cdr (cdr p))))\n```\n\nThen when I initialize the environment, I do:\n```scheme\n(env-define! env '+ (make-primitive '+ +))  \n```\n\nWait, but `+` in the outer environment IS the Python function. So `+` in the `call-primitive` call would also be the tagged primitive...\n\nHmm, I need to break this circularity. The trick is that the eval.scm code itself (the functions `my-eval`, `my-apply`, etc.) uses the HOST environment (interp.py's global env). So when the eval.scm code does `(define (my-apply proc args) ...)`, the body of `my-apply` is compiled in interp.py's environment.\n\nBut the CODE BEING EVALUATED by my-eval uses a different environment (the one I create). So the key is: in my `call-primitive`, I dispatch based on the primitive name and call the corresponding HOST primitive function. The `+` I use in this dispatch is the HOST `+`, not the one from my evaluator's environment.\n\nWait, but if I write `(+ a b)` in my eval.scm, and eval.scm is loaded by interp.py, then `+` IS the Python function. It's only in the evaluator's environment (the one I create for evaluated code) that `+` is mapped to something else.\n\nSo `call-primitive` is part of eval.scm's code, which runs in interp.py's environment. When `call-primitive` does `(+ (car args) (car (cdr args)))`, the `+` here is interp.py's `+`, which IS the Python function.\n\nThis works!\n\nBut wait - let me trace through the self-interpretation case more carefully.\n\nOuter level: interp.py runs eval.scm\n- interp.py creates global env with Python primitives\n- interp.py loads eval.scm - defines functions like my-eval, my-apply, call-primitive, init-env, etc.\n- interp.py evaluates the top-level code of eval.scm: `(eval-program ...)`\n- This opens a file (eval.scm itself) and reads/evaluates expressions using my-eval\n- The environment for these expressions is created by `init-env`, which maps `+` to a tagged primitive\n\nInner level: my-eval evaluates expressions from eval.scm\n- It evaluates `(define (my-eval expr env) ...)` etc.\n- These are function definitions in the new environment\n- Then it evaluates the top-level body of the inner eval.scm: `(eval-program ...)`\n- `eval-program` calls `read` - which is a tagged primitive\n- my-apply dispatches to `call-primitive` with name 'read\n- `call-primitive` does `(read)` - but `read` here is interp.py's `read`! Wait, no...\n\nHmm, actually `call-primitive` is a function defined by eval.scm. Its body runs in the context of interp.py's global environment (for the outer level). But when eval.scm is being interpreted by my-eval (inner level), the inner level's `call-primitive` is also being interpreted by the outer my-eval.\n\nWhen the outer my-eval evaluates the expression `(define (call-primitive name args) ...)`, it creates a procedure with the body of `call-primitive`. When this procedure is called (in the inner evaluator), the body expressions are evaluated in the procedure's closure environment.\n\nThe body of `call-primitive` does something like:\n```scheme\n((eq? name '+) (+ (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n```\n\nWhen the inner evaluator applies this, it needs to evaluate `(+ (car args) (car (cdr args)))`. The `+` is a symbol, which my-eval looks up in the procedure's environment. But the procedure was defined by the outer evaluator, which had `+` mapped to... hmm.\n\nWait, let me be more precise. When the outer my-eval evaluates `(define (call-primitive name args) ...)`, it creates a Procedure object with:\n- params: (name args)\n- body: the body expressions of call-primitive\n- env: the outer evaluator's environment (created by init-env, where `+` is a tagged primitive)\n\nWhen this Procedure is called, its body expressions are evaluated in a new environment that extends the closure environment. So `+` is looked up in the closure environment, which was the outer evaluator's environment, where `+` is a tagged primitive.\n\nSo when `call-primitive` tries to do `(+ (car args) (car (cdr args)))`, it looks up `+` and finds the tagged primitive. Then `my-apply` is called with the tagged primitive, which dispatches to `call-primitive` again → infinite recursion!\n\nThis is the fundamental problem. I need a way to access the HOST primitives from within the evaluated code.\n\nSolution: I need to keep the host primitives accessible in the evaluator's environment, but under different names. Or I need to implement the primitives purely in Scheme without relying on the host.\n\nActually, the cleanest solution is: don't use a dispatch table. Scheme uses lexical scoping. `apply-binary-+` doesn't capture `+` at definition time; `+` is looked up when the function is called.\n\nIn a lexically scoped language like Scheme, `(define (apply-binary-+ args) (+ (car args) (car (cdr args))))` - the body `(+ (car args) (car (cdr args)))` is evaluated in the environment where `apply-binary-+` was defined. The evaluated expressions are separate from the evaluator code.\n\nThe issue here is that the evaluator code (eval.scm) IS Scheme code in the same language. Let me think...\n\nActually, here's the approach:\n\nFor primitives, I tag them as `(list 'primitive proc)`. Then in my `apply` function:\n```scheme\n(define (apply proc args)\n  (if (and (pair? proc) (eq? (car proc) 'primitive))\n      ;; It's a primitive - need to call it\n      (apply-primitive (cdr proc) args)\n      ;; It's a user-defined procedure\n      (apply-user-proc proc args)))\n```\n\nFor `apply-primitive`, I need to call `proc` with the arguments from `args`. But `args` is a list, and I need to destructure it.\n\nFor unary primitives:\n```scheme\n(define (apply-primitive-1 proc args)\n  (proc (car args)))\n```\n\nFor binary primitives:\n```scheme\n(define (apply-primitive-2 proc args)\n  (proc (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n```\n\nBut this doesn't work because `proc` is a Python function, and when I try to call `(proc arg)` from within my Scheme code, the host interpreter:\n1. Evaluates `proc` → gets the Python function (from the environment lookup)\n2. Evaluates `arg` → gets the value\n3. Sees it's callable and calls it!\n\nWait, does this actually work? Let me trace through:\n\n```scheme\n(define (apply-primitive proc args)\n  (proc (car args)))\n```\n\nWhen this is called like `(apply-primitive + (cons 1 (cons 2 '())))`:\n1. Host evaluates `apply-primitive` → gets my compound procedure\n2. Host evaluates `+` → gets the Python + function\n3. Host evaluates `(cons 1 (cons 2 '()))` → gets a Pair(1, Pair(2, None))\n4. Host creates a new env and binds `proc` → Python + function, `args` → Pair(1, Pair(2, None))\n5. Host evaluates `(proc (car args))` in the new env\n6. Host evaluates `proc` → gets the Python + function (from the local env)\n7. Host evaluates `(car args)` → gets 1\n8. Host evaluates `(proc (car args))` → Python + with arg 1\n\nWait, but `(proc (car args))` is an application form. The host evaluates it:\n- `proc` → Python + function  \n- `(car args)` → 1\n- The host has a callable (the Python +) and one argument (1)\n- It calls `+(1)` → this returns 1 (since `-` has unary behavior: `-a`)\n\nBut I want `+(1, 2)`, not `+(1)`. I need to pass BOTH arguments.\n\nOK so I need to handle arity manually. For binary primitives, I'd do:\n```scheme\n(proc (car args) (car (cdr args)))\n```\n\nAnd for n-ary:\nI'd need to write separate wrappers for each arity.\n\nBut this is getting complex. Let me think of a simpler approach.\n\nActually wait - what if I DON'T distinguish between primitives and compound procedures and instead just use the host's native calling convention? Constructs `(proc 1 2)` → NO, can't construct arbitrary expressions\n\nOK, another approach: instead of having my own eval/apply that handles everything, what if I just REPLACE the evaluator with a simpler approach?\n\nWhat if my eval.scm just does:\n1. No, there's no eval in the language.\n\nWait - actually, maybe I should look at this problem differently. The core issue is: how does my Scheme code apply a primitive (Python function) to a list of arguments?\n\nLet me look at what actual operations are available to me:\n\n1. `+` - variadic sum\n2. `-` - unary or binary subtraction\n3. `*` - variadic product\n4. `/` - binary division\n5. `=` - binary equality\n6. `<`, `>`, `<=`, `>=` - binary comparisons\n7. `cons` - binary pair constructor\n8. `car` - unary pair accessor\n9. `cdr` - unary pair accessor\n10. `null?` - unary null check\n11. `pair?` - unary pair check\n12. `eq?` - binary equality\n13. `equal?` - binary equality\n14. `not` - unary negation\n15. `and`, `or` - variadic logical ops\n16. `getchr` - zero-argument char input\n17. `putchr` - unary char output\n18. `chr` - unary number to char\n19. `read` - zero-argument read S-expression\n20. `symbol` - unary list of char codes to symbol\n21. `symbol?`, `string?`, `number?` - unary type checks\n22. `set-car!`, `set-cdr!` - binary mutation\n23. `display` - unary output\n24. `newline` - zero-argument newline\n25. `fopen`, `fclose`, `fgetchr`, `fputchr`, `fdisplay`, `fread` - file I/O\n\nThe trick is: when I write `(proc a b)` in my Scheme code (where proc is bound to a Python function), the host interpreter DOES evaluate it correctly. It calls the Python function with the given arguments.\n\nSo the question is: in my eval function, when I've determined that the operator is a primitive (Python function), how do I INVOKE it?\n\nHere's the thing: I CAN invoke it, but only with a fixed number of arguments that I spell out in my source code. If I have the arguments as a list, I need to destructure them.\n\nFor a 0-ary primitive like `newline`:\n```scheme\n(proc)\n```\n\nFor a 1-ary primitive like `display`:\n```scheme\n(proc (car args))\n```\n\nFor a 2-ary primitive like `cons`:\n```scheme\n(proc (car args) (car (cdr args)))\n```\n\nFor a 3-ary... In this implementation, they're functions, so all arguments are evaluated before the function is called. This means `(and #f (/ 1 0))` would cause a division by zero error. When a line is read, it tokenizes it and accumulates tokens. When parens balance, it parses and returns. This means that multiple S-expressions on separate lines or on one line both work.\n\nBut there's an issue: `fread` reads ONE expression and returns. For the next expression, you call `fread` again. This is different from reading all expressions at once. My `process-file` loop handles this correctly by calling `fread` in a loop.\n\nBut there's a problem: the last expression in the file might not be followed by a newline. Let me check `fread`... it uses `readline()` which returns the line including the newline character. On the last line without a trailing newline, `readline()` still returns the content. On EOF, it returns `\"\"`. So this should work fine.\n\nActually, let me look more carefully at fread's logic for lines without parens:\n\nIf a line contains just `(+ 1 2)` (10 chars), `readline()` returns `\"(+ 1 2)\\n\"` or `\"(+ 1 2)\"`. Tokenizing gives `(`, `+`, `1`, `2`, `)`. paren_count goes 0→1→1→1→1→0. Since tokens is non-empty and paren_count is 0, it parses and returns `(+ 1 2)`. Good.\n\nFor multiple expressions on separate lines: each call to `fread` reads one line, parses one expression, and returns. The next call reads the next line.\n\nFor multiple expressions on the same line: like `(+ 1 2)(- 3 4)`. Tokenized: `(`, `+`, `1`, `2`, `)`, `(`, `-`, `3`, `4`, `)`. paren_count goes 0→1→1→1→1→0→... wait, at the first `)`, paren_count becomes 0 and tokens is non-empty, so fread returns `(+ 1 2)`. But there are still remaining tokens on the same line! The next call to fread reads the NEXT LINE, and the `(- 3 4)` tokens are lost!\n\nThis is a bug in fread. But looking at `interp.py`'s approach, it tokenizes all lines at once and then parses. So for evaluation, multiple expressions on one line work because the whole file is tokenized at once.\n\nFor `fread`, this is a known limitation. But looking at the test files, they all have one expression per line or expressions split across lines, never multiple expressions on one line. So it should be fine.\n\nActually, let me reconsider. The test files DO have potential issues. For example, from test/01-factorial.scm:\n\n```\n(display (factorial 5))\n(newline)\n```\n\nThese are on separate lines, so each call to `fread` reads one expression. Good.\n\nBut wait, what about:\n```\n(define (fib-list n)\n  (define (fib-list-helper i)\n    (if (>= i n)\n        '()\n        (cons (fib i) (fib-list-helper (+ i 1)))))\n  (fib-list-helper 0))\n```\n\nThis is one expression spanning multiple lines. `fread` reads line 1, tokens are `(` `define` `(` `fib-list` `n` `)`. paren_count = 2. Not balanced, so it reads the next line: `(` `define` `(` `fib-list-helper` `i` `)`. paren_count increases further. It keeps reading until all parens are balanced. Good, this works.\n\nOK, I'm now confident that my approach will work. Let me write the eval.scm file.\n\nLet me also think about what happens when `fread` encounters `'()` (quoted empty list). Tokenization: `'` `(` `)`. The `'` triggers quote handling in parse_expr, so it becomes `(quote ())`. But wait, `()` is parsed as None. So `'()` becomes `(Pair 'quote (Pair None None))`. In my eval, this evaluates to `None` (since quote just returns the value). This is correct.\n\nNow, let me think about one more thing. In my eval, when I look up a symbol in the environment, I use `env-lookup`. But the host's global environment has the primitives. My eval creates its own environment. I need to make sure my environment includes all primitives.\n\nIn my `setup-global-env` function, I'll define all the primitives. The tricky part is that I'm accessing them from the HOST's global environment. Since eval.scm's code runs in the host, when I write `+` in my code, it refers to the host's `+` function. So:\n\n```scheme\n(define (setup-global-env)\n  (let ((env (cons '() '())))\n    (env-define! env '+ +)\n    (env-define! env '- -)\n    ...\n    env))\n```\n\nThe `+` on the right side of `env-define!` refers to the host's `+` binding. When `env-define!` stores it in my env's alist, it stores the actual Python function. Later, when my eval does `(env-lookup env '+)`, it returns this Python function. Then `apply` checks if it's a user-defined procedure (it's not, since it's not a pair), and calls `apply-primitive` which calls `(proc (car args) (car (cdr args)))` which invokes the Python function correctly.\n\nThis will work!\n\nLet me now also think about the `my-length` function I'll need. I need it for arity checking:\n\n```scheme\n(define (my-length lst)\n  (if (null? lst)\n      0\n      (+ 1 (my-length (cdr lst)))))\n```\n\nAnd for `reverse`:\n\n```scheme\n(define (reverse lst)\n  (define (reverse-iter lst acc)\n    (if (null? lst)\n        acc\n        (reverse-iter (cdr lst) (cons (car lst) acc))))\n  (reverse-iter lst '()))\n```\n\nAnd for `list` / making lists:\n\n```scheme\n;; Construct a list from elements: (list a b c) = (cons a (cons b (cons c '())))\n;; But we can't define variadic functions.\n;; We'll construct lists manually using cons.\n```\n\nOK, I think I have enough understanding to write the code. Let me plan the file structure:\n\n1. **Helper functions**: make-procedure, my-length, reverse, assoc\n2. **Environment functions**: make-env, extend-env, env-lookup, env-define!, env-set!\n3. **Procedure representation**: procedure-params, procedure-body, procedure-env\n4. **Eval special form handlers**: eval-if, eval-define, eval-set!, eval-lambda, eval-begin, eval-let, eval-cond\n5. **Evaluation helpers**: eval-body, eval-args, eval-application\n6. **Apply**: apply, apply-primitive, apply-user-proc\n7. **Main eval function**: eval\n8. **Environment setup**: setup-global-env\n9. **Main program**: Read file path, open file, create env, process expressions\n\nOne concern: the arity check for compound procedures. In `interp.py`, compound procedures check arity and raise an error if it doesn't match. For my metacircular evaluator, I should do the same.\n\nAlso, I need to think about recursion limits. `interp.py` has a recursion limit of 10000. My Scheme code might hit Python's recursion limit when deeply nested. But that's a runtime issue, not a design issue.\n\nOne more thing: when eval.scm evaluates expressions from the file, the evaluated results shouldn't be displayed automatically (only `display` and `newline` should produce output). Looking at `interp.py`'s `run_file`, it evaluates expressions but doesn't print the results. This is what my eval should do too.\n\nOK, let me now actually write the code. Let me be comprehensive and handle all the primitives.\n\nLet me also think about whether I need `while` or any loop construct. Since I'm using recursion throughout, I don't need `while`. \n\nOne thing I need to be careful about: the order of definitions matters in Scheme. Since functions can reference each other, I need to make sure that nested functions can reference sibling functions (like in the mutual recursion example). Since my `eval` processes definitions sequentially in the same environment, this works: after `define even?`, `even?` is in the env; after `define odd?`, `odd?` is in the env. Both are available when the final body expression runs.\n\nBut wait - what about the case where `even?` references `odd?` in its body? When `even?` is defined, `odd?` doesn't exist yet. But `even?`'s body isn't evaluated until it's called. At call time, both `even?` and `odd?` are in the environment (since all top-level defines in a function are evaluated before the body). So mutual recursion works!\n\nThe key insight is that nested defines (inside a function body) are evaluated as part of the body, and they populate the same environment. So by the time the last body expression runs, all definitions are in place.\n\nLet me double-check this with `interp.py`:\n\nIn `interp.py`, when defining a function like `(define (f x) (define a 1) (define b 2) (+ a b))`:\n1. The function shorthand creates a Procedure with body = `[(define a 1), (define b 2), (+ a b)]`\n2. When applied, it creates a new env and evaluates each body expression in order\n3. First `(define a 1)` adds `a` to the env\n4. Then `(define b 2)` adds `b` to the env\n5. Then `(+ a b)` uses both\n\nThis works because the body is evaluated sequentially. My evaluator does the same thing.\n\nNow wait, there's actually a subtle issue. In standard Scheme, all internal defines in a function body are \"lifted\" to the top of the function, so they're all defined before any body expression runs. This enables mutual recursion:\n\n```scheme\n(define (f x)\n  (define (even? n) (if (= n 0) #t (odd? (- n 1))))\n  (define (odd? n) (if (= n 0) #f (even? (- n 1))))\n  (even? x))\n```\n\nIn `interp.py`, this works because:\n1. Body is `[(define (even? n) ...), (define (odd? n) ...), (even? x)]`\n2. Step 1: defines even? in the env (its body references odd? but doesn't evaluate it yet)\n3. Step 2: defines odd? in the env (its body references even?, which IS in the env now)\n4. Step 3: calls (even? x), which works because both are defined\n\nSo sequential evaluation of defines in the function body DOES support mutual recursion, as long as one function doesn't call the other during its definition (which it doesn't, since the body is just stored as unevaluated data).\n\nGreat, so my approach matches interp.py's behavior.\n\nNow let me write the actual code. I'll structure it carefully.\n\n```scheme\n;;\n;; eval.scm - Metacircular Evaluator\n;;\n;; Reads one line from STDIN (a file path), interprets the .scm file at that path,\n;; and redirects remaining STDIN to the interpreted program.\n;;\n\n;; ============================================================\n;; Helper functions\n;; ============================================================\n\n(define (length lst)\n  (if (null? lst)\n      0\n      (+ 1 (length (cdr lst)))))\n\n(define (reverse lst)\n  (define (iter lst acc)\n    (if (null? lst)\n        acc\n        (iter (cdr lst) (cons (car lst) acc))))\n  (iter lst '()))\n\n(define (assoc key alist)\n  (cond\n    ((null? alist) #f)\n    ((eq? key (car (car alist))) (car alist))\n    (else (assoc key (cdr alist)))))\n\n;; ============================================================\n;; Environment operations\n;; ============================================================\n;; An environment is a pair (frame . parent)\n;;   frame: alist of (name . value) pairs\n;;   parent: another environment or '() for the global env\n\n(define (make-env parent)\n  (cons '() parent))\n\n(define (extend-env params args env)\n  (define (bind params args)\n    (if (null? params)\n        '()\n        (cons (cons (car params) (car args))\n              (bind (cdr params) (cdr args)))))\n  (cons (bind params args) env))\n\n(define (env-lookup env name)\n  (let ((binding (assoc name (car env))))\n    (if (not (eq? binding #f))\n        (cdr binding)\n        (if (null? (cdr env))\n            (error \"undefined: \" name)\n            (env-lookup (cdr env) name)))))\n\n(define (env-define! env name value)\n  (set-car! env (cons (cons name value) (car env))))\n\n(define (env-set! env name value)\n  (let ((binding (assoc name (car env))))\n    (if (not (eq? binding #f))\n        (set-cdr! binding value)\n        (if (null? (cdr env))\n            (error \"undefined: \" name)\n            (env-set! (cdr env) name value)))))\n\n;; ============================================================\n;; Procedure representation\n;; ============================================================\n;; A compound procedure is a list: (procedure params body env)\n;; Constructed as: (cons 'procedure (cons params (cons body (cons env '()))))\n\n(define (make-procedure params body env)\n  (cons 'procedure (cons params (cons body (cons env '())))))\n\n(define (procedure-params proc) (car (cdr proc)))\n(define (procedure-body proc) (car (cdr (cdr proc))))\n(define (procedure-env proc) (car (cdr (cdr (cdr proc)))))\n\n(define (compound-procedure? proc)\n  (and (pair? proc) (eq? (car proc) 'procedure)))\n\n;; ============================================================\n;; Evaluation helpers\n;; ============================================================\n\n(define (eval-body body env)\n  (if (null? body)\n      (if #f #f)  ; return unspecified\n      (if (null? (cdr body))\n          (eval (car body) env)\n          (progn\n            (eval (car body) env)\n            (eval-body (cdr body) env)))))\n\n(define (eval-args args env)\n  (if (null? args)\n      '()\n      (cons (eval (car args) env)\n            (eval-args (cdr args) env))))\n\n(define (eval-application expr env)\n  (let ((proc (eval (car expr) env))\n        (args (eval-args (cdr expr) env)))\n    (apply proc args)))\n\n;; Special form handlers\n\n(define (eval-if expr env)\n  (if (eval (car (cdr expr)) env)\n      (eval (car (cdr (cdr expr))) env)\n      (if (not (null? (car (cdr (cdr (cdr expr))))))\n          (eval (car (cdr (cdr (cdr expr)))) env)\n          (if #f #f))))\n\n(define (eval-define expr env)\n  (let ((name-or-pair (car (cdr expr))))\n    (if (pair? name-or-pair)\n        ;; Function shorthand: (define (f x y) body1 body2 ...)\n        (let ((name (car name-or-pair))\n              (params (cdr name-or-pair))\n              (body (cdr (cdr expr))))\n          (env-define! env name (make-procedure params body env)))\n        ;; Simple definition: (define name value)\n        (env-define! env name-or-pair (eval (car (cdr (cdr expr))) env))))\n  (if #f #f))\n\n(define (eval-set! expr env)\n  (env-set! env (car (cdr expr)) (eval (car (cdr (cdr expr))) env))\n  (if #f #f))\n\n(define (eval-lambda expr env)\n  (let ((params (car (cdr expr)))\n        (body (cdr (cdr expr))))\n    (make-procedure params body env)))\n\n(define (eval-begin expr env)\n  (eval-body (cdr expr) env))\n\n(define (eval-cond expr env)\n  (define (eval-clauses clauses)\n    (if (null? clauses)\n        (if #f #f)\n        (let ((clause (car clauses)))\n          (let ((test (car clause)))\n            (if (or (eq? test 'else) (eval test env))\n                (eval-body (cdr clause) env)\n                (eval-clauses (cdr clauses)))))))\n  (eval-clauses (cdr expr)))\n\n(define (eval-let expr env)\n  (let ((bindings (car (cdr expr)))\n        (body (cdr (cdr expr))))\n    (define (extract-params b)\n      (if (null? b)\n          '()\n          (cons (car (car b)) (extract-params (cdr b)))))\n    (define (extract-args b)\n      (if (null? b)\n          '()\n          (cons (eval (car (cdr (car b))) env) (extract-args (cdr b)))))\n    (let ((params (extract-params bindings))\n          (args (extract-args bindings)))\n      (let ((new-env (extend-env params args env)))\n        (eval-body body new-env)))))\n\n;; ============================================================\n;; Apply\n;; ============================================================\n\n(define (apply proc args)\n  (if (compound-procedure? proc)\n      (let ((params (procedure-params proc))\n            (body (procedure-body proc))\n            (proc-env (procedure-env proc)))\n        (if (= (length params) (length args))\n            (let ((new-env (extend-env params args proc-env)))\n              (eval-body body new-env))\n            (error \"arity mismatch\")))\n      (apply-primitive proc args)))\n\n(define (apply-primitive proc args)\n  (cond\n    ((null? args) (proc))\n    ((null? (cdr args)) (proc (car args)))\n    ((null? (cdr (cdr args))) (proc (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n    ((null? (cdr (cdr (cdr args)))) (proc (car args) (car (cdr args)) (car (cdr (cdr args)))))\n    ((null? (cdr (cdr (cdr (cdr args))))) \n     (proc (car args) (car (cdr args)) (car (cdr (cdr args))) (car (cdr (cdr (cdr args))))))\n    ((null? (cdr (cdr (cdr (cdr (cdr args))))))\n     (proc (car args) (car (cdr args)) (car (cdr (cdr args))) (car (cdr (cdr (cdr args)))) (car (cdr (cdr (cdr (cdr args)))))))\n    (else (error \"too many args\"))))\n\n;; ============================================================\n;; Main eval function\n;; ============================================================\n\n(define (eval expr env)\n  (cond\n    ((number? expr) expr)\n    ((string? expr) expr)\n    ((eq? expr #t) #t)\n    ((eq? expr #f) #f)\n    ((symbol? expr) (env-lookup env expr))\n    ((not (pair? expr)) expr)  ; other self-evaluating\n    (else\n      (let ((op (car expr)))\n        (cond\n          ((eq? op 'quote) (car (cdr expr)))\n          ((eq? op 'if) (eval-if expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'define) (eval-define expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'set!) (eval-set! expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'lambda) (eval-lambda expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'let) (eval-let expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'begin) (eval-begin expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'progn) (eval-begin expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'cond) (eval-cond expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'or) (eval-or expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'and) (eval-and expr env))\n          (else (eval-application expr env)))))))\n\n;; Wait, 'or' and 'and' are primitives in the host language.\n;; But in my eval, if I treat them as applications, they'll work too.\n;; Actually, evaluating '(or a b c)' as an application would evaluate all args\n;; and then call the 'or' primitive which does any(args). This works the same\n;; as directly calling the primitive.\n;; \n;; But wait - in standard Scheme, 'or' and 'and' are special forms that short-circuit.\n;; In this language, they're implemented as functions: (or a b) evaluates both a and b\n;; and then calls any([a, b]). So treating them as applications is correct since\n;; all args are already evaluated.\n\n;; Actually, I DON'T need eval-or and eval-and since treating them as applications\n;; works correctly. Let me remove those cases from the cond.\n\nLet me remove the or/and special cases:\n\n```scheme\n(define (eval expr env)\n  (cond\n    ((number? expr) expr)\n    ((string? expr) expr)\n    ((eq? expr #t) #t)\n    ((eq? expr #f) #f)\n    ((symbol? expr) (env-lookup env expr))\n    ((not (pair? expr)) expr)\n    (else\n      (let ((op (car expr)))\n        (cond\n          ((eq? op 'quote) (car (cdr expr)))\n          ((eq? op 'if) (eval-if expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'define) (eval-define expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'set!) (eval-set! expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'lambda) (eval-lambda expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'let) (eval-let expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'begin) (eval-begin expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'progn) (eval-begin expr env))\n          ((eq? op 'cond) (eval-cond expr env))\n          (else (eval-application expr env)))))))\n```\n\nNow, the setup function:\n\n```scheme\n(define (setup-global-env)\n  (let ((env (cons '() '())))\n    ;; Arithmetic\n    (env-define! env '+ +)\n    (env-define! env '- -)\n    (env-define! env '* *)\n    (env-define! env '/ /)\n    (env-define! env '= =)\n    (env-define! env '< <)\n    (env-define! env '> >)\n    (env-define! env '<= <=)\n    (env-define! env '>= >=)\n    \n    ;; Pair operations\n    (env-define! env 'cons cons)\n    (env-define! env 'car car)\n    (env-define! env 'cdr cdr)\n    (env-define! env 'null? null?)\n    (env-define! env 'pair? pair?)\n    \n    ;; Equality\n    (env-define! env 'eq? eq?)\n    (env-define! env 'equal? equal?)\n    \n    ;; Logical\n    (env-define! env 'not not)\n    (env-define! env 'and and)\n    (env-define! env 'or or)\n    \n    ;; I/O\n    (env-define! env 'getchr getchr)\n    (env-define! env 'putchr putchr)\n    (env-define! env 'read read)\n    (env-define! env 'display display)\n    (env-define! env 'newline newline)\n    (env-define! env 'chr chr)\n    \n    ;; Type predicates\n    (env-define! env 'symbol? symbol?)\n    (env-define! env 'string? string?)\n    (env-define! env 'number? number?)\n    \n    ;; Mutation\n    (env-define! env 'set-car! set-car!)\n    (env-define! env 'set-cdr! set-cdr!)\n    \n    ;; Other\n    (env-define! env 'symbol symbol)\n    \n    ;; File I/O\n    (env-define! env 'fopen fopen)\n    (env-define! env 'fclose fclose)\n    (env-define! env 'fgetchr fgetchr)\n    (env-define! env 'fputchr fputchr)\n    (env-define! env 'fdisplay fdisplay)\n    (env-define! env 'fread fread)\n    \n    ;; else for cond\n    (env-define! env 'else #t)\n    \n    env))\n```\n\nWait, there's an issue. When I set up the global environment for the inner interpreter, the symbols like `+`, `-`, etc. on the RIGHT side of `env-define!` refer to the bindings in the OUTER (host) environment. But wait - do they?\n\nIn the `setup-global-env` function, when I write `(env-define! env '+ +)`, the `+` on the right is evaluated in the HOST environment, because `setup-global-env` is running in the host. So it gets the Python `+` function. This is then stored in the new env.\n\nBut when my eval.scm interprets itself, the inner eval's `setup-global-env` is called. The `+` inside it... hmm, this is the tricky part. When the inner eval defines `setup-global-env`, this function is a Procedure in the inner environment. When it's called, `+` is looked up in the inner environment. But at that point, `+` might not be in the inner environment yet (we're in the process of setting it up!).\n\nWait, no. When `setup-global-env` is called, it creates a new env. The `+` symbol inside the function body is resolved in the environment where `setup-global-env` was defined, which is the INNER environment. At the time the inner eval defines `setup-global-env`, the inner environment has all the definitions from eval.scm plus... hmm, actually, the `+` symbol in `setup-global-env`'s body is just a reference to the host's `+` binding.\n\nWait, no. The `setup-global-env` function is defined in eval.scm. When it's evaluated by the HOST, the `+` symbol in its body resolves to the HOST's `+`. So even in the self-interpreting case, the inner eval's `setup-global-env` function body has `+` which was resolved by the HOST (because the function was created by the HOST's lambda).\n\nActually, no. Let me think more carefully. When eval.scm is interpreted:\n\n**First level (host interprets eval.scm):**\n- Host defines `setup-global-env` as a compound procedure.\n- The compound procedure's env is the HOST's global environment.\n- The body includes `(env-define! env '+ +)`. The `+` here is a SYMBOL in the body.\n- When `setup-global-env` is called, the host creates a new env extending the HOST's env.\n- The host evaluates `+` (looking it up in the HOST env) → gets Python + function.\n- `env-define!` stores it. Correct.\n\n**Second level (inner eval from self-interpretation):**\n- Inner eval's process reads eval.scm and defines `setup-global-env` as its own Procedure.\n- This Procedure's env is the INNER eval's environment.\n- The body includes `(env-define! env '+ +)`. The `+` here is a SYMBOL in the body.\n- When `setup-global-env` is called by the inner eval:\n  - Inner eval's `eval` processes the application.\n  - `setup-global-env` is a compound procedure in the inner env.\n  - `apply` creates a new env extending the procedure's env (the inner env).\n  - Inner eval's `eval` processes `(env-define! env '+ +)`.\n  - It evaluates `env-define!` → gets the inner env's `env-define!` function.\n  - It evaluates `env` → gets the new env.\n  - It evaluates `'+` → symbol 'quote... wait, no, `'+` is `(quote +)`.\n\n Actually, let me look at what `(env-define! env '+ +)` parses to. It's:\n\n`(Pair 'env-define! (Pair 'env (Pair (Pair 'quote (Pair '+ '())) (Pair '+ '()))))`\n\nWait no. The expression `(env-define! env '+ +)` has three arguments after `env-define!`: `env`, `'+`, and `+`.\n\n`'+` → `(quote +)` → the symbol `+`\n`+` → the symbol `+`\n\nSo the third arg is the symbol `+` and the fourth arg is the symbol `+`. When `setup-global-env` runs in the host:\n- Third arg `'+` is the `quote` special form, returning the symbol `+`.\n- Fourth arg `+` is a lookup of the symbol `+`, returning the Python function.\n\nWhen `setup-global-env` runs in the inner eval:\n- Inner eval processes the application `(env-define! env '+ +)`.\n- Operator: `env-define!` → the inner eval's `env-define!` function.\n- Args: `env`, `'+`, `+`.\n  - `env` → evaluated in the inner env → the new env.\n  - `'+` → the quote special form, returns the symbol `+`.\n  - `+` → looked up in the inner env → the Python + function (which was put there by setup).\n\nBut wait! At the time `setup-global-env` is running (setting up the inner env), the inner environment doesn't have `+` yet! We're in the process of adding it!\n\nThis is a chicken-and-egg problem. The `setup-global-env` function needs `+` (and other primitives) to be in the environment it runs in, but it's trying to set up that environment.\n\nHmm, but in the HOST-level execution, `setup-global-env`'s body contains literal symbols like `+` that are resolved in the function's closure environment, not the environment being set up. The closure environment for `setup-global-env` is the environment where it was defined.\n\nAt the HOST level: `setup-global-env` is defined in the HOST's global env, where `+` is a Python function. From the Scheme side, how do I differentiate? \n\nLooking at the Python code:\n- `Procedure` has a `__repr__` that returns `#<procedure>`\n- Python functions (lambdas) will have some string representation\n\nBut from the Scheme side, I can't easily check if something is a primitive vs a user procedure. (cdr args))\n         (- (car args))  ; unary negation\n         (- (car args) (car (cdr args)))))  ; binary sub\n    ((eq? name '*) (* (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n    ((eq? name 'car) (car (car args)))\n    ((eq? name 'cdr) (cdr (car args)))\n    ((eq? name 'cons) (cons (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n    ((eq? name 'display) (display (car args)))\n    ((eq? name 'read) (read))\n    ((eq? name 'newline) (newline))\n    ...))\n```\n\nTHIS WORKS! It can't handle interp.py Procedure objects.\n\nSo the inner evaluator would fail in step 4: it has a value (an interp.py Procedure object) and tries to apply it, but its apply only knows how to handle tagged lists.\n\nOK so the wrapper approach doesn't work for the meta-circular case either (at least, not directly).\n\nThe fundamental problem: in the metacircular evaluator, when the inner evaluator encounters a Python primitive or an interp.py Procedure in the environment, it can't apply them because they're not in its format.\n\nLet me now think about what actually needs to happen for the metacircular case to work.\n\nIn the metacircular case:\n- The outer evaluator processes inner eval.scm\n- The inner eval.scm defines its own evaluator functions\n- The inner evaluator processes the final test program\n\nThe inner evaluator's environment needs to contain ONLY things it can handle. '+ '+)` is `#t`, so `(= '+ '+)` = `#t`\n   e. `(display (+ (car (cdr line)) (car (cdr (cdr line)))))`:\n      - `(cdr line)` is `(7 8)`, `(car (cdr line))` is `7`\n      - `(cdr (cdr line))` is `(8)`, `(car (cdr (cdr line)))` is `8`\n      - `(+ 7 8)` = `15`\n      - `(display 15)` → prints \"15\"\n\nWhen run through eval.scm:\n1. interp.py loads eval.scm and evaluates its top-level forms\n2. eval.scm's main code: `(read)` → gets `test/calculator.scm` as a symbol\n3. Evaluating `+` → `prim:+` Procedure\n   b. The interp.py Procedure is NOT a pair—it's its own Python object type. Actually, what IS an interp.py Procedure?\n\nLooking at the Procedure class:\n```python\nclass Procedure:\n    def __init__(self, params, body, env):\n        self.params = params\n        self.body = body\n        self.env = env\n```\n\nAnd from Python, `isinstance(proc, Procedure)` is True for these objects. But from Scheme, how does this appear?\n\nIn the Scheme language (interp.py), what does `(pair? proc)` return for a Procedure? Let me check: `pair?` is implemented as `isinstance(x, Pair)`. Procedure is NOT a subclass of Pair, so `(pair? proc)` returns `#f`.\n\nSo from Scheme:\n- `(pair? some-procedure)` → `#f`\n- `(number? some-procedure)` → `#f`\n- `(string? some-procedure)` → `#f`\n- `(symbol? some-procedure)` → `#f`\n- `(null? some-procedure)` → `#f`\n\nWhat does `(eq? some-procedure some-procedure)` return? Since `eq?` uses Python `==`, and Procedure doesn't define `__eq__`, Python's default identity check is used. So `(eq? proc1 proc2)` is `#f` for different Procedure objects.\n\nHmm, but can I check if something is a Procedure from Scheme? There's no `procedure?` primitive. But I COULD add one to interp.py... no, I can't modify interp.py.\n\nOK so in my evaluator, how do I distinguish between:\n1. Are the wrappers created as interp.py Procedures? Let me re-check. In eval.scm:\n\n```scheme\n(define (prim:+ a b) (+ a b))\n```\n\ninterp.py's eval processes this `define`. I'm writing `(my-apply proc args)` where `proc` is the value. (car proc) 'primitive))\n     (apply-native-primitive (car (cdr proc)) args))\n    ((and (pair? proc) (eq? When I write `(+ (car args) (car (cdr args)))` in my Scheme code, interp.py evaluates this EXPRESSION. It calls `+` with the results of `(car args)` and `(car (cdr args))`. Since `+` is an interp.py primitive and `car` is an interp.py primitive, this works.\n\nSo the solution is:\n1. DON'T store Python functions/interp.py Procedures in my environment\n2. Instead, store tagged symbols like `'(primitive +)` that identify which primitive to call\n3. In my-apply, when I see a primitive marker, I use a giant cond that explicitly calls the right Python primitive\n4. The cond branches are evaluated by interp.py, which can actually call Python primitives\n\nYES! This should work perfectly.\n\nLet me verify for the self-interpreting case:\n\n**Outer evaluator (Level 1) processes inner eval.scm (Level 2)**\n\nThe outer evaluator's global-env has `+` → `'(primitive +)`, `car` → `'(primitive car)`, etc.\n\nWhen processing inner eval.scm's `(define (prim:+ a b) (+ a b))`:\n1. my-eval processes `define`:\n   - Creates tagged procedure: `'(procedure (a b) ((+ a b)) <current-env>)`\n   - current-env = outer evaluator's global-env (where `+` → `'(primitive +)`)\n   - Binds `prim:+` to this procedure in current-env\n\n2. Evaluates the three arguments:\n   - `inner-global` → gets the inner env (from lookup)\n   - `'+` → the symbol `+` (quote is a special form in my evaluator)\n   - `prim:+` → gets the tagged procedure (from lookup)\n3. Looks up `+` in its env → tagged procedure from the inner global env\n2. This tagged procedure has body `((+ a b))` and env pointing to outer global env\n\nWait, no! Let me re-check. The inner eval.scm defines `prim:+` as:\n```scheme\n(define (prim:+ a b) (+ a b))\n```\n\nThe outer evaluator processes this. At this point, the \"current environment\" is the inner eval's global environment. So:\n- The procedure `prim:+` is created with env = inner eval's global environment\n- After definition, `prim:+` is stored in inner eval's global environment\n- The body of `prim:+` is `((+ a b))`\n\nWhen evaluating the body `(+ a b)`, the `+` is looked up in the current env (which chains to the procedure's env = inner eval's global environment).\n\nIn the inner eval's global environment, `+` was defined by the inner eval.scm's `(env-define inner-global '+ prim:+)`. So `+` points to the inner `prim:+` procedure (the tagged list).\n\nWhen applying inner `prim:+` to `(3 4)`:\n- New env: a=3, b=4, parent = procedure's env (inner global env)\n- Evaluate body `(+ a b)`: look up `+` in current env → inner global env → inner `prim:+`\n- Apply inner `prim:+` → infinite loop!\n\nThat's infinite recursion! `prim:+` calls itself!\n\nThe problem: when defining the wrapper `prim:+` as `(lambda (a b) (+ a b))`, the `+` in the body refers to the `+` in the current environment. In the top level, `+` was just defined as `prim:+`. So the body `(+ a b)` calls `prim:+` again, leading to infinite recursion.\n\nThe issue is that in the inner eval.scm, when we define `prim:+`, the `+` in the body `(+ a b)` is resolved to whatever `+` is in the current environment. If `+` was already set to `prim:+` (which it will be, since we're initializing the environment), then calling `prim:+` calls itself.\n\nWait, but in the inner eval.scm, the environment is initialized from scratch:\n```scheme\n(define inner-global (make-env '()))\n```\n\nInitially empty. Then:\n\n```scheme\n(env-define inner-global '+ prim:+)\n(env-define inner-global '- prim:-)\n...\n```\n\nBut `prim:+` itself is defined in the inner eval.scm's code as:\n```scheme\n(define (prim:+ a b) (+ a b))\n```\n\nWhen the outer evaluator processes this `define`, it evaluates the lambda expression `(lambda (a b) (+ a b))` in the current environment. The current environment is the one we pass to my-eval. If we're processing top-level forms, this is... the inner global env? But the inner global env might NOT have `+` bound yet at the time this `define` is processed!\n\nWait, `+` in the body of `prim:+` is a SYMBOL. When the outer evaluator creates the tagged procedure for `prim:+`, it stores the unevaluated expression `( + a b )` (well, actually it stores `( (+ a b) )` which is a list containing the application expression). The `+` is a symbol in the expression. It's NOT evaluated at definition time.\n\nWhen `prim:+` is APPLIED later, the outer evaluator evaluates the body `(+ a b)` in a new environment. The `+` is looked up in this new environment (which chains to the procedure's env → inner global env).\n\nAt that point (when `prim:+` is actually called, AFTER all environment initialization is done), `+` should be bound in the inner global env. IF the initialization code was run BEFORE any calls happen.\n\nBut here's the thing: `+` is bound to `prim:+` in the inner global env. So `(+ a b)` → evaluate body of `prim:+` → `(+ a b)` → evaluate body of `prim:+` → ... INFINITE.\n\nThe problem is that `prim:+`'s body references `+`, which resolves to `prim:+` itself.\n\nIn the OUTER eval.scm, this isn't a problem because `(define (prim:+ a b) (+ a b))` is evaluated by interp.py. interp.py's `define` creates a Procedure whose env is interp.py's global env. In interp.py's global env, `+` is the Python `+` function. So no infinite recursion.\n\nBut when the OUTER evaluator (my-eval) processes the INNER eval.scm's `(define (prim:+ a b) (+ a b))`, the tagged procedure's env is the inner global env (where `+` → inner `prim:+`). So the body `(+ a b)` resolves `+` to inner `prim:+`, causing infinite recursion.\n\nOK so this is the problem with the wrapper approach in the metacircular case. The solution: DON'T define `prim:+` as `(lambda (a b) (+ a b))` at all. Instead, use the primitive dispatch approach.\n\nHere's the revised plan:\n\n1. In eval.scm, for EACH primitive, I have a direct hard-coded branch in apply:\n```scheme\n(define (apply-primitive name args)\n  (cond\n    ((eq? name '+) (+ (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n    ((eq? name '-) \n     (if (null? (cdr args))\n         (- (car args))  ; unary\n         (- (car args) (car (cdr args)))))  ; binary\n    ((eq? name 'cons) (cons (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n    ((eq? name 'car) (car (car args)))\n    ((eq? name 'cdr) (cdr (car args)))\n    ((eq? name 'display) (display (car args)))\n    ((eq? name 'newline) (newline))\n    ((eq? name 'read) (read))\n    ((eq? name 'null?) (null? (car args)))\n    ((eq? name 'pair?) (pair? (car args)))\n    ((eq? name 'symbol?) (symbol? (car args)))\n    ((eq? name 'number?) (number? (car args)))\n    ((eq? name 'string?) (string? (car args)))\n    ((eq? name 'eq?) (eq? (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n    ; ... etc for all primitives\n  ))\n```\n\n2. In the global environment, store markers: `(define + '(primitive +))`\n\n3. In my-apply: check for primitive marker, dispatch to apply-primitive\n\n4. For user-defined procedures: check for '(procedure ...) tagged list, do the normal apply\n\nThis avoids the infinite recursion problem because:\n- In the outer eval, `apply-primitive` is a Scheme function defined by `(define (apply-primitive name args) ...)` in eval.scm\n- This function is evaluated by interp.py\n- When interp.py evaluates the body of apply-primitive, it evaluates things like `(+ (car args) (car (cdr args)))` using its own eval\n- `+` inside apply-primitive's body is looked up in interp.py's global environment → Python `+` function\n- This works because apply-primitive is defined at the top level of eval.scm, which interp.py evaluates\n\nFor the self-interpreting case:\n- Outer evaluator processes inner eval.scm\n- Inner eval.scm also defines `(define (apply-primitive name args) ...)`\n- When the outer evaluator encounters this define, it creates a tagged procedure\n- When the inner evaluator needs to apply `+`, it calls `apply-primitive` with name `'+` and args\n- `apply-primitive`'s body is `(cond ... ((eq? name '+) (+ (car args) (car (cdr args)))) ...)`\n- The outer evaluator evaluates this body (since apply-primitive is a tagged procedure)\n- Inside the body, `+` is looked up in the current env → inner global env → outer `apply-primitive`'s... wait, no\n\nHmm, there's still a potential issue. Like:\n\n```scheme\n; For + (variable arity, at least 1)\n(define (apply-plus args)\n  (if (null? (cdr args))\n      (car args)\n      (+ (car args) (apply-plus (cdr args)))))\n```\n\nWhen the meta evaluator applies `+` (represented as a primitive tagged procedure), it calls `(apply-plus args)` which recursively calls `+` through the host with exactly 2 args each time.\n\nThis is the key! I can handle variable-arity primitives by converting them to fixed-arity calls through the host.\n\nFor `*`: similar to `+`, iteratively multiply.\n\nFor `-`: `(- a b)` subtracts, `(- a)` negates.\n\nFor `/`: `(/ a b)` divides.\n\nLet me think about which primitives have variable arity:\n- `+`: variable arity (2 in practice, but supports any number)\n- `*`: variable arity\n- `-`: `(- a)` or `(- a b)` (1 or 2 args)\n- `/`: `(/ a b)` (2 args)\n- `and`: variable arity\n- `or`: variable arity\n\nAll others have fixed arity.\n\nActually, looking at interp.py more carefully:\n- `+` uses `sum(args)` - works with any number including 0\n- `*` uses eval with string join - works with any number \n- `-` takes 1 or 2 args\n- `/` takes 2 args\n- `and` and `or` use `all`/`any` - variable arity\n\nFor the meta evaluator approach, I think the cleanest solution is:\n\n1. Let me go with this.\n\nNow let me plan the full evaluator:\n\n### Data structures:\n\n**Environment**: A list of frames, each frame is an alist.\n```\nenv = (frame . parent-env)\nframe = ((name . value) ...)\n```\n\n**User-defined procedure**: A tagged list\n```\n(cons 'procedure (cons params (cons body env)))\n```\nwhere:\n- `params` is a list of symbols e.g. `(x y)`\n- `body` is a list of expressions e.g. `((+ x y))`\n- `env` is the closure environment\n\n**Primitive procedure**: A tagged list\n```\n(cons 'primitive name)\n```\nwhere `name` is a symbol like `'+`\n\n### Functions needed:\n\n1. `make-env` - create new empty environment\n2. `extend-env` - extend environment with new frame\n3. `env-define!` - define in current frame\n4. `env-set!` - set! searching up\n5. `env-lookup` - lookup searching up\n6. `eval` - evaluate expression\n7. `eval-list` - evaluate a list of expressions\n8. `apply` - apply procedure to arguments\n9. `apply-primitive` - apply primitive procedure\n10. `setup-global-env` - create global environment with primitives\n\n### Special forms to handle:\n\n1. `quote` - return expression as-is\n2. `if` - conditional\n3. `define` - create binding (including function shorthand)\n4. `set!` - mutate binding\n5. `lambda` - create procedure\n6. `let` - local bindings (desugar to lambda application)\n7. `begin` / `progn` - sequencing\n8. `cond` - conditional chain\n\n### Primitives to wrap:\n\n- `cons`, `car`, `cdr`, `null?`, `pair?`, `eq?`, `equal?`, `set-car!`, `set-cdr!`\n- `symbol?`, `string?`, `number?`\n- `not`, `and`, `or`\n- `+`, `-`, `*`, `/`, `=`, `<`, `>`, `<=`, `>=`\n- `display`, `newline`, `getchr`, `putchr`, `chr`\n- `read`, `symbol`\n- `fopen`, `fclose`, `fgetchr`, `fputchr`, `fdisplay`, `fread`\n\nLet me also check: do I need `apply-primitive` for `and` and `or`? All the actual work happens at the HOST level.\n\nThis is correct! The meta evaluator's apply function calls `apply-primitive` which is just a regular Scheme function that the HOST evaluates. The HOST handles all primitive calls within `apply-primitive`.\n\nNow, `apply-primitive` needs to return the result to the meta evaluator. The function `apply-primitive` takes a primitive name and a list of args (already evaluated). It dispatches to the right handler based on the name. In the meta evaluator, I also need to handle `define` with function shorthand. The shorthand `(define (f x y) (body))` should expand to `(define f (lambda (x y) (body)))`.\n\nIn interp.py:\n```python\nelif car == \"define\":\n    name_or_list = expr.cdr.car\n    if isinstance(name_or_list, Pair):\n        # Function definition shorthand\n        name = name_or_list.car\n        params = []\n        param_list = name_or_list.cdr\n        while param_list is not None:\n            params.append(param_list.car)\n            param_list = param_list.cdr\n        body = []\n        body_list = expr.cdr.cdr\n        while body_list is not None:\n            body.append(body_list.car)\n            body_list = body_list.cdr\n        proc = Procedure(params, [], env)\n        proc.body = body\n        env.define(name, proc)\n```\n\nThe params list is used directly. The HOST handles everything.\n\nFor variable arity, define wrapper functions at eval.scm's top level that use fixed-arity calls:\n\n```scheme\n(define (plus-help args)\n  (if (null? args)\n      0\n      (if (null? (cdr args))\n          (car args)\n          (+ (car args) (plus-help (cdr args))))))\n```\n\nBut when the meta evaluator evaluates `(+ (car args) (plus-help (cdr args)))`, we're back to the same issue with `+`.\n\nLet me try a completely different approach:\n\n**The meta evaluator wraps primitives as tagged objects. For each primitive, the meta evaluator's apply function knows how to apply it by calling a specific helper function that uses FIXED-ARITY HOST calls.**\n\nThe helper functions are defined at eval.scm's top level and called directly by the meta evaluator's apply function. These helper functions use HOST-level primitives ONLY THROUGH FIXED-ARITY CALLS that go through the HOST eval.\n\nWait, I think I need to take a step back and think about what actually happens in the META EVALUATOR'S APPLY function.\n\nLet me define the meta evaluator's `apply` function body:\n\n```scheme\n(define (apply-proc proc args)\n  (cond\n    ((is-user-defined-proc? proc)\n     ; Create new env, evaluate body\n     (let ((new-env (extend-env (proc-env proc) (proc-params proc) args)))\n       (eval-body (proc-body proc) new-env)))\n    ((is-primitive? proc)\n     ; Handle primitive\n     (apply-primitive (primitive-name proc) args))\n    (else\n     ; Unknown\n     'error)))\n```\n\nNow, `apply-primitive` returns the result of the primitive call. For each primitive:\n\n```scheme\n(define (apply-primitive name args)\n  (cond\n    ((eq? name '+) (plus-helper args))\n    ((eq? name '-) (minus-helper args))\n    ((eq? name 'cons) (prim-cons (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n    ...))\n```\n\nWhere `plus-helper` is:\n```scheme\n(define (plus-helper args)\n  (if (null? args)\n      0\n      (if (null? (cdr args))\n          (car args)\n          (plus-helper-2 (car args) (plus-helper (cdr args))))))\n\n(define (plus-helper-2 a b)\n  (host-+ a b))\n```\n\nWhere `host-+` is defined at eval.scm's top level:\n```scheme\n(define (host-+ a b) (+ a b))\n```\n\nNow, when the meta evaluator's apply function calls `(plus-helper args)`:\n1. The HOST evaluates this expression\n2. The HOST calls plus-helper (a user-defined procedure, but in the HOST's eval, it's just a Procedure)\n3. plus-helper recursively reduces to `(plus-helper-2 a b)` where a and b are numbers\n4. The HOST evaluates `(plus-helper-2 a b)` → calls plus-helper-2\n5. Inside plus-helper-2: `(host-+ a b)` → the HOST evaluates this\n6. host-+ is a user-defined procedure, body is `(+ a b)`\n7. The HOST evaluates `(+ a b)` → finds `+` in HOST global env → Python function\n8. Python function is callable → HOST calls it\n\nWait, but when the META evaluator calls `(plus-helper args)`... actually, I realize now that ALL of this is happening at the HOST level, not the meta evaluator level!\n\nLet me trace more carefully:\n\n1. The meta evaluator's `eval` function sees `(+ 1 2 3)` in sub-code\n2. Meta evaluator evaluates `+` → tagged primitive `(primitive . +)` from meta env\n3. Meta evaluator evaluates args `1`, `2`, `3` → `(1 2 3)`\n4. Meta evaluator calls `(apply-proc tagged-+ '(1 2 3))`\n5. In `apply-proc`, cond branch: is-primitive? → yes\n6. Calls `(apply-primitive '+, '(1 2 3))`\n7. In `apply-primitive`, dispatch: `(eq? name '+)` → calls `(plus-helper '(1 2 3))`\n\nThe thing is: step 7 is a regular Scheme function call in the meta evaluator code. The HOST evaluates it. `plus-helper` is a user-defined Procedure. The HOST creates an env, binds `args` to `(1 2 3)`, and evaluates the body.\n\nThe body of `plus-helper` is an `if` expression: `(if (null? args) 0 (if (null? (cdr args)) (car args) (plus-helper-2 (car args) (plus-helper (cdr args)))))`.\n\nThe HOST evaluates this. `(null? args)` → `(null? '(1 2 3))` → looks up `null?` in HOST global env → Python function. The Python `null?` function checks `x is None`. `'(1 2 3)` is not None, so returns `#f`.\n\nSince the condition is `#f` (false), the HOST evaluates the else branch: `(if (null? (cdr args)) ...)`.\n\n`(null? (cdr args))` → `(null? '(2 3))` → #f. So we go to the else: `(plus-helper-2 (car args) (plus-helper (cdr args)))`.\n\n`plus-helper` calls `(plus-helper '(2 3))`. This again goes through the HOST. Eventually we get to `(plus-helper-2 2 3)`.\n\n`plus-helper-2` has body `(host-+ a b)`. The HOST evaluates this. It looks up `host-+` → user-defined Procedure. Body: `(+ a b)`. The HOST evaluates `(+ a b)`. It looks up `+` → Python function from HOST global env. It evaluates `a` → 2, `b` → 3. Now it applies: the procedure is a Python function, callable. The HOST calls `+(2, 3)` → 5.\n\nSo the flow is:\n```\nMeta eval sub-code (+ 1 2 3)\n  → Meta apply-proc tagged-+ (1 2 3)\n    → Meta apply-primitive + (1 2 3)  [HOST evaluates this call]\n      → HOST calls plus-helper (1 2 3)\n        → HOST repeatedly calls plus-helper, plus-helper-2\n          → HOST evaluates (+ a b) in host-+ body\n            → HOST finds Python +, calls it\n            → Returns 5\n```\n\nThe key insight: `plus-helper`, `plus-helper-2`, `host-+` are all evaluated by the HOST directly. They are NOT evaluated by the meta evaluator. So the Python functions within their bodies are also handled by the HOST.\n\nThis means:\n- `plus-helper` is a regular Scheme function at eval.scm's top level\n- Its body uses `null?`, `cdr`, `car`, etc. - all of which are HOST-level Python functions\n- The HOST handles these directly\n- `plus-helper-2` uses `(host-+ a b)` which eventually uses the HOST's `+`\n\nWait, but `plus-helper`'s body `(if (null? args) ...)` uses `null?`, `car`, `cdr`. These are Python functions from the HOST's global environment. The HOST evaluates these expressions directly and calls the Python functions. So `null?` checks for None, `car` gets the first element, etc. All fine.\n\nBut wait - in `plus-helper`, we're using Scheme constructs like `if`, `null?`, `cdr`, `car`. These are being evaluated by the HOST. The `if` is a special form handled by interp.py. `null?`, `cdr`, `car` are Python functions in the HOST's global env. The HOST handles all of these.\n\nSo `plus-helper` is essentially just a regular Scheme function that the HOST interprets. It doesn't go through the meta evaluator at all!\n\nThis means the meta evaluator's `apply-primitive` function acts as a BRIDGE:\n1. The meta evaluator identifies that a procedure is a primitive\n2. It calls `apply-primitive` which dispatches to the right helper\n3. The helper is evaluated entirely by the HOST\n4. The result is returned to the meta evaluator\n\nThis is exactly the same pattern as SICP's metacircular evaluator! In SICP:\n```scheme\n(define (apply-primitive-procedure proc args)\n  (apply-in-underlying-scheme\n   (primitive-implementation proc) args))\n```\n\nThe `apply-in-underlying-scheme` is the HOST's apply, which directly applies the primitive.\n\nIn our case, the \"underlying Scheme's apply\" is the HOST environment's ability to handle function calls. We don't have a general `apply`, but we can simulate it with explicit helpers for each primitive.\n\nOK, so actually, we CAN simplify this. Instead of having both `apply-primitive` AND separate helpers, the meta evaluator's `apply` function can directly use HOST-level calls for each primitive.\n\nBut wait - `apply-primitive` runs inside the meta evaluator. When `apply-proc` calls `(apply-primitive name args)`, the HOST evaluates this because `apply-proc` is running under the HOST. So `apply-primitive`'s body, which dispatches on `name`, is evaluated by the HOST.\n\nAnd `(plus-helper args)` in `apply-primitive` is also evaluated by the HOST. So `plus-helper` is a HOST-level call.\n\nBut `plus-helper` returns a VALUE to `apply-primitive`, which returns it to `apply-proc`, which returns it to `eval`, which returns it to the caller. So the result flows back through the meta evaluator's call chain.\n\nOK I think I've worked this out enough. The approach works. Let me write the actual code now.\n\nHere's the final structure:\n\n```scheme\n;; eval.scm - Metacircular evaluator\n\n;; ========= Helper primitives (evaluated by HOST) =========\n\n(define (host-+ a b) (+ a b))\n(define (host-- a b) (- a b))\n(define (host-* a b) (* a b))\n(define (host-/ a b) (/ a b))\n(define (host-= a b) (= a b))\n(define (host-< a b) (< a b))\n(define (host-> a b) (> a b))\n(define (host-<= a b) (<= a b))\n(define (host->= a b) (>= a b))\n\n(define (plus-helper args)\n  (if (null? args)\n      0\n      (if (null? (cdr args))\n          (car args)\n          (host-+ (car args) (plus-helper (cdr args))))))\n\n(define (minus-helper args)\n  (if (null? (cdr args))\n      (host-- (car args))\n      (host-- (car args) (car (cdr args)))))\n\n(define (mult-helper args)\n  (if (null? args)\n      1\n      (if (null? (cdr args))\n          (car args)\n          (host-* (car args) (mult-helper (cdr args))))))\n\n(define (div-helper args)\n  (host-/ (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n\n... etc\n\n;; ========= Environment =========\n\n(define (make-env parent)\n  (cons '() parent))\n\n(define (env-define! env name val)\n  (set-car! env (cons (cons name val) (car env))))\n\n(define (env-lookup env name)\n  (cond ((null? env) 'error)\n        ((assoc name (car env))\n         (cdr (assoc name (car env))))\n        (else (env-lookup (cdr env) name))))\n\n(define (assoc key alist)\n  (cond ((null? alist) #f)\n        ((eq? key (car (car alist))) (car alist))\n        (else (assoc key (cdr alist)))))\n```\n\nHmm wait, I'm using `assoc` recursively, and `env-lookup` calls `assoc` and then `cdr` on the result. But I'm calling `assoc` TWICE - once for the check and once for the result. Let me use a let:\n\n```scheme\n(define (env-lookup env name)\n  (if (null? env)\n      'error\n      (let ((binding (assoc name (car env))))\n        (if binding\n            (cdr binding)\n            (env-lookup (cdr env) name)))))\n```\n\nBut `let` evaluates the value before binding. And I need `assoc` to search the current frame.\n\nActually, `let` in interp.py:\n```python\nelif car == \"let\":\n    new_env = Environment(env)\n    bindings = expr.cdr.car\n    while bindings is not None:\n        binding = bindings.car\n        name = binding.car\n        value = eval_expr(binding.cdr.car, env)\n        new_env.define(name, value)\n        bindings = bindings.cdr\n\n    result = None\n    body = expr.cdr.cdr\n    while body is not None:\n        result = eval_expr(body.car, new_env)\n        body = body.cdr\n    return result\n```\n\nSo `let` evaluates all bindings in the OUTER env, creates a new child env, then evaluates the body in the new child. Standard Scheme `let`.\n\nNow, `(let ((binding (assoc name (car env)))) ...)` - this evaluates `(assoc name (car env))` in the current environment and binds `binding` to the result. This should work.\n\nLet me now write the full code. I need to be careful about:\n\n1. All primitives need to be handled\n2. The meta evaluator needs to handle its own special forms\n3. The file I/O needs to work correctly\n\nLet me also think about what happens with `define` inside procedure bodies (nested defines). These need to define in the current (inner) environment.\n\nIn the meta evaluator's `eval` function:\n```scheme\n((eq? op 'define) (eval-define expr env))\n```\n\nAnd `eval-define`:\n```scheme\n(define (eval-define expr env)\n  (let ((defn (car (cdr expr))))\n    (if (pair? defn)\n        ; Function shorthand\n        (let ((name (car defn))\n              (params (cdr defn))\n              (body (cdr (cdr expr))))\n          (env-define! env name\n            (cons 'procedure (cons params (cons body env)))))\n        ; Simple define\n        (let ((name defn)\n              (val (eval (car (cdr (cdr expr))) env)))\n          (env-define! env name val)))))\n```\n\nThis should work. When inside a lambda body, `env` is the new environment created by the procedure application. Defining in this env creates bindings local to that call.\n\nOK, now let me handle one more issue: In the meta evaluator's `apply` function for user-defined procedures, I need to handle the case where the body has multiple expressions. I also need to handle the `define` special form within the body.\n\nActually, `define` within a body is just another expression that the meta evaluator's `eval` function handles. As long as the procedure application evaluates all body expressions in order, it works.\n\nLet me now also think about `set!`:\n\n```scheme\n((eq? op 'set!) \n (let ((name (car (cdr expr)))\n       (val (eval (car (cdr (cdr expr))) env)))\n   (env-set! env name val)))\n```\n\nAnd `env-set!`:\n```scheme\n(define (env-set! env name val)\n  (cond ((null? env) 'error)\n        ((assoc name (car env))\n         => ???)\n        (else (env-set! (cdr env) name val))))\n```\n\nWait, I can't use `=>` in cond. Let me use let:\n\n```scheme\n(define (env-set! env name val)\n  (if (null? env)\n      'error\n      (let ((binding (assoc name (car env))))\n        (if binding\n            (set-cdr! binding val)\n            (env-set! (cdr env) name val)))))\n```\n\nThis mutates the binding in the first frame that contains it.\n\nSimilarly for `env-lookup`:\n```scheme\n(define (env-lookup env name)\n  (if (null? env)\n      'error\n      (let ((binding (assoc name (car env))))\n        (if binding\n            (cdr binding)\n            (env-lookup (cdr env) name)))))\n```\n\nNow let me also handle `display` and `newline` primitives. These are side-effect operations.\n\n`display` in the HOST: `lambda x: print(str(x) if isinstance(x, String) else x, end=\"\")`\n\nIn the meta evaluator, I need to call the HOST's `display` with one argument.
