Primeval... still same problem\n\nOK, I think the FUNDAMENTAL issue is that ANY time a user-defined procedure's body is evaluated by the meta evaluator, any primitive calls in that body will go through the meta evaluator's apply, which doesn't handle Python functions.\n\nThe only way around this is to NOT have user-defined procedures whose bodies are evaluated by the meta evaluator. Instead, all the primitive wrappers should be defined so that the HOST handles their body evaluation.\n\nBut all user-defined procedures are going to have their bodies evaluated by the meta evaluator (that's the point of the meta evaluator!).\n\nSo the real question is: how to make the meta evaluator's apply handle Python functions?\n\nTHE ANSWER: The meta evaluator's `apply` function can call Python functions using a special form. Specifically:\n\n```scheme\n(define (apply-proc proc args)\n  (cond\n    ((is-user-defined? proc) (apply-user-proc proc args))\n    ((is-primitive? proc) (apply-primitive-proc proc args))\n    (else 'error)))\n```\n\nWhere `apply-primitive-proc` calls the primitive directly:\n\n```scheme\n(define (apply-primitive-proc proc args)\n  ; proc is a tagged primitive (primitive . name)\n  ; use the helper for the specific primitive\n  (let ((name (cdr proc)))\n    (cond\n      ((eq? name '+) (plus-helper args))\n      ((eq? name '-) (minus-helper args))\n      ((eq? name 'cons) (cons (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n      ((eq? name 'car) (car (car args)))\n      ((eq? name 'cdr) (cdr (car args)))\n      ((eq? name 'null?) (null? (car args)))\n      ((eq? name 'pair?) (pair? (car args)))\n      ((eq? name 'display) (display (car args)))\n      ((eq? name 'newline) (newline))\n      ...)))\n```\n\nWait, now `display`, `newline`, `cons`, `car`, `cdr`, `null?`, `pair?` in THIS context are Python functions from the HOST's global environment! Because `apply-primitive-proc` is a top-level function in eval.scm, and it's evaluated by the HOST.\n\nSo when the HOST evaluates `(display (car args))` inside `apply-primitive-proc`:\n1. `display` → Python function from HOST global env\n2. `(car args)` → first element of args list\n3. HOST applies `display` to the result of `(car args)` → calls Python function\n\nThis works because the HOST handles the entire evaluation of `apply-primitive-proc`.\n\nBut then I can simplify by NOT having tags at all! Instead of tagged primitives, I can directly store the Python functions. And in the meta evaluator's apply function, after checking for user-defined procedures, I just... call the Python function directly?\n\nBut the problem is, how do I call a Python function with a dynamic number of args from a variable?\n\nLet me think again...\n\nFor FIXED-ARITY primitives, I can hardcode the calls in `apply-proc`:\n\n```scheme\n(define (apply-proc proc args)\n  (cond\n    ((is-user-defined? proc) ...)\n    ((null? args) (proc))          ; 0-arg primitives\n    ((null? (cdr args)) (proc (car args)))  ; 1-arg primitives\n    ((null? (cdr (cdr args))) (proc (car args) (car (cdr args))))  ; 2-arg primitives\n    (else 'error)))\n```\n\nWait, but `(proc (car args))` - the HOST evaluates this. It looks up `proc` as a symbol... no, `proc` is a PARAMETER of `apply-proc`. When the HOST evaluates `(proc (car args))`, it's in the form of a function application: car is `proc`, cdr is `((car args))`. The HOST evaluates the car of the list: `proc` → the Python function object. The HOST evaluates the cdr: `(car args)` → value. The HOST sees that `proc` is callable → calls it with the value.\n\nYES! This DOES work! The HOST evaluates `(proc (car args))` and `proc` IS in scope because it's a parameter. The HOST creates an environment where `proc` is bound to the Python function. So the HOST can look up `proc` and get the function.\n\nSo the meta evaluator's apply function can handle Python functions like this! But there's still the arity problem: I need to know the arity at compile time.\n\nBut for primitives, I KNOW the arities:\n- 0 args: `getchr` (hmm, actually `getchr` reads one char), `newline`, `read`\n- 1 arg: `car`, `cdr`, `null?`, `pair?`, `symbol?`, `string?`, `number?`, `not`, `display`, `chr`\n- 2 args: `+`, `-`, `*`, `/`, `=`, `<`, `>`, `<=`, `>=`, `cons`, `eq?`, `equal?`, `set-car!`, `set-cdr!`, `fopen`, `fclose`, `fputchr`\n- 3 args: `fdisplay`\n- Variable: `+`, `-`, `*`, `and`, `or` (but `+`, `*` are binary in practice with more args being folded)\n\nWait, `+` has variable arity. If sub-code calls `(+ 1 2 3)`, the meta evaluator evaluates 3 args. Then it needs to call `proc` with 3 args. Using the fixed-arity dispatch:\n\n```scheme\n(let ((len (length args)))\n  (cond\n    ...))\n```\n\nBut `length` isn't a built-in! I need to implement it.\n\nActually, I can implement `length`:\n```scheme\n(define (my-length lst)\n  (if (null? lst)\n      0\n      (+ 1 (my-length (cdr lst)))))\n```\n\nBut `+` in the body is the HOST's `+`, so this is evaluated by the HOST (since `my-length` is a top-level function called by the HOST).\n\nActually wait, I need to be more careful about what's evaluated by the HOST vs. the meta evaluator.\n\nTop-level functions in eval.scm (like `my-length`, `apply-proc`, `plus-helper`, etc.) are all evaluated by the HOST when they're called. They're defined with `define` at the top level, which interp.py processes. When these functions are called, the HOST evaluates their bodies.\n\nBut in the meta evaluator, user-defined procedures (created by the meta evaluator's `eval-lambda`) are evaluated by the meta evaluator when their body is evaluated.\n\nSo the split is:\n- HOST-evaluated: all top-level functions in eval.scm (the meta evaluator's implementation)\n- Meta-evaluated: all procedures created by the meta evaluator (from interpreted sub-code)\n\nThe bridge functions (like `plus-helper`) must be HOST-evaluated. Which means they must be regular top-level Scheme functions called by the meta evaluator's apply function. The meta evaluator's apply function calls them (as HOST-level calls), and the HOST evaluates them.\n\nSo the structure is:\n1. Meta evaluator's `eval` and `apply` handle all special forms and procedure application\n2. For user-defined procedures, the meta evaluator creates environments and evaluates bodies\n3. For primitives, the meta evaluator's `apply` calls HOST-level bridge functions\n4. The bridge functions use HOST-level primitives (Python functions) and return results to the meta evaluator\n\nThis is the correct architecture. Let me code it up now.\n\nFor `apply-proc`, I'll handle Python functions by checking their arity:\n\nActually wait, I need to distinguish between Python functions and user-defined procedures in the meta evaluator. The user-defined procedures are tagged: `(cons 'procedure (cons params (cons body env)))`. Python functions are just Python function objects.\n\nI can check if something is a user-defined procedure by checking if it's a pair and its car is 'procedure:\n\n```scheme\n(define (user-defined? proc)\n  (and (pair? proc) (eq? (car proc) 'procedure)))\n```\n\nThen in `apply-proc`:\n```scheme\n(define (apply-proc proc args)\n  (cond\n    ((user-defined? proc) (apply-user-proc proc args))\n    (else (apply-primitive proc args))))  ; Python function\n```\n\nBut wait, `args` is a list (Pairs). For a Python function `+`, I need to pass all args. I can't call `(proc arg1 arg2 arg3)` from Scheme... UNLESS I use the fixed-arity dispatch.\n\nActually, let me go with the tagged primitive approach after all. It's cleaner:\n\nIn the meta evaluator, I store primitives as tagged Pairs: `(cons 'primitive '+)`. Then in `apply-proc`:\n\n```scheme\n(define (apply-proc proc args)\n  (cond\n    ((user-defined? proc) (apply-user-proc proc args))\n    ((and (pair? proc) (eq? (car proc) 'primitive)) \n     (apply-primitive (cdr proc) args))\n    (else 'error)))\n```\n\nAnd `apply-primitive` dispatches to HOST-level helpers:\n\n```scheme\n(define (apply-primitive name args)\n  (cond\n    ((eq? name '+) (plus-helper args))\n    ((eq? name '-) (minus-helper args))\n    ((eq? name '*) (mult-helper args))\n    ((eq? name 'cons) (host-cons (car args) (car (cdr args))))\n    ((eq? name 'car) (host-car (car args)))\n    ((eq? name 'cdr) (host-cdr (car args)))\n    ...))\n```\n\nWhere `host-cons`, `host-car`, `host-cdr` are top-level wrappers:\n```scheme\n(define (host-cons a d) (cons a d))\n(define (host-car p) (car p))\n(define (host-cdr p) (cdr p))\n```\n\nAnd `plus-helper`:\n```scheme\n(define (plus-helper args)\n  (if (null? args)\n      0\n      (if (null? (cdr args))\n          (car args)\n          (host-+ (car args) (plus-helper (cdr args))))))\n```\n\nThese HOST-level wrappers call the Python functions directly through interp.py.\n\nThis approach is clean and works for all primitives. Let me code the whole thing now.\n\nActually, I realize I need to handle one more thing: `apply-primitive` gets called by the meta evaluator's `apply-proc` function. So `apply-primitive` is a HOST-evaluated function, and its body dispatches to HOST-level helpers. Perfect.\n\nNow let me think about what needs to be handled:\n\n### Primitives (to tag and handle):\n\n**0-arg:**\n- `newline` → (newline)\n- `read` → (read)\n- `getchr` → (getchr)\n\n**1-arg:**\n- `car`, `cdr`, `null?`, `pair?`, `symbol?`, `string?`, `number?`\n- `not`, `display`, `chr`\n- `fclose`\n\n**2-arg:**\n- `cons`, `eq?`, `equal?`, `set-car!`, `set-cdr!`\n- `=`, `<`, `>`, `<=`, `>=`\n- `fopen`, `fputchr`\n- `-`, `/` (and `+`, `*` for binary case)\n\n**3-arg:**\n- `fdisplay`\n\n**Variable:**\n- `+`, `*`, `-`, `and`, `or`\n\nWait, actually, let me check: does `apply-primitive` need to handle `and` and `or`? In interp.py, these are defined as:\n```python\nenv.define(\"and\", lambda *args: all(args))\nenv.define(\"or\", lambda *args: any(args))\n```\n\nThey're just Python functions. In the meta evaluator, when evaluating `(and a b)`:\n1. Evaluate `and` → tagged primitive `(primitive . and)`\n2. Evaluate `a`, `b` → values\n3. The `f` in the function position gets evaluated, and if it's a callable/Pair, it gets applied.\n\nSo I could define fixed-arity apply functions:\n\n```scheme\n(define (apply0 proc) (proc))\n(define (apply1 proc a) (proc a))\n(define (apply2 proc a b) (proc a b))\n(define (apply3 proc a b c) (proc a b c))\n(define (apply4 proc a b c d) (proc a b c d))\n(define (apply5 proc a b c d e) (proc a b c d e))\n(define (apply6 proc a b c d e f) (proc a b c d e f))\n(define (apply7 proc a b c d e f g) (proc a b c d e f g))\n(define (apply8 proc a b c d e f g h) (proc a b c d e f g h))\n```\n\nAnd then in apply-proc, count the args and use the right one:\n\n```scheme\n(define (apply-func proc args)\n  (cond\n    ((null? args) (apply0 proc))\n    ((null? (cdr args)) (apply1 proc (car args)))\n    ((null? (cddr args)) (apply2 proc (car args) (cadr args)))\n    ((null? (cdddr args)) (apply3 proc (car args) (cadr args) (caddr args)))\n    ...))\n```\n\nThis is a bit verbose but it works! Let me now write the full evaluator.\n\n### Full plan\n\n```scheme\n;;; eval.scm - Metacircular evaluator for Scheme-like language\n\n;;; Helper functions for list access\n(define (caar x) (car (car x)))\n(define (cadr x) (car (cdr x)))\n(define (cdar x) (cdr (car x)))\n(define (cddr x) (cdr (cdr x)))\n(define (caadr x) (car (car (cdr x))))\n(define (caddr x) (car (cdr (cdr x))))\n(define (cdadr x) (cdr (car (cdr x))))\n(define (cdddr x) (cdr (cdr (cdr x))))\n(define (cadddr x) (car (cdr (cdr (cdr x)))))\n(define (cddddr x) (cdr (cdr (cdr (cdr x)))))\n\n;;; Environment operations\n(define (make-env parent) (cons '() parent))\n\n(define (env-define env name value)\n  (set-car! env (cons (cons name value) (car env))))\n\n(define (env-lookup env name)\n  (if (null? env)\n      (error \"undefined:\" name)\n      (let ((frame (car env)))\n        (let ((found (assoc name frame)))\n          (if found\n              (cdr found)\n              (env-lookup (cdr env) name))))))\n\n(define (env-set! env name value)\n  (if (null? env)\n      (error \"set! undefined:\" name)\n      (let ((found (assoc name (car env))))\n        (if found\n            (set-cdr! found value)\n            (env-set! (cdr env) name value)))))\n\n(define (assoc key alist)\n  (cond\n    ((null? alist) #f)\n    ((eq? key (caar alist)) (car alist))\n    (else (assoc key (cdr alist)))))\n\n;;; Evaluate a list of arguments, returning a list of values\n(define (eval-args arg-list env)\n  (if (null? arg-list)\n      '()\n      (cons (eval-expr (car arg-list) env)\n            (eval-args (cdr arg-list) env))))\n\n;;; Sequence evaluation (for begin/progn and lambda bodies)\n(define (eval-seq body env)\n  (if (null? body)\n      '()\n      (if (null? (cdr body))\n          (eval-expr (car body) env)\n          (progn\n            (eval-expr (car body) env)\n            (eval-seq (cdr body) env)))))\n\n;;; Apply user-defined procedure\n(define (apply-user-proc proc args)\n  (let ((params (cadr proc))\n        (body (caddr proc))\n        (closure (cadddr proc)))\n    (let ((new-env (make-env closure)))\n      (define (bind i)\n        (if (null? i)\n            'done\n            (progn\n              (env-define new-env (car i) (car args))\n              (bind (cdr i)))))\n      ;; Wait, I need both params and args...\n      ...))))\n```\n\nHmm, I need both `params` and `args` in the binding loop. (car proc) 'procedure)\n         (apply-user-proc proc args)\n         ;; Pair that's not a procedure - try calling it\n         (error \"not a procedure\")))\n    ((number? proc) (error \"not a procedure:\" proc))\n    ((string? proc) (error \"not a procedure:\" proc))\n    ((symbol? proc) (error \"not a procedure:\" proc))\n    ((eq? proc #t) (error \"not a procedure:\" proc))\n    ((eq? proc #f) (error \"not a procedure:\" proc))\n    ((null? proc) (error \"not a procedure:\" proc))\n    (else\n     ;; Must be a primitive - call it with args\n     (cond\n       ((null? args) (proc))\n       ((null? Contains 'procedure-tagged lists for user-defined functions.\n\nWhen my evaluator evaluates `(factorial 5)`, it looks up `factorial` in its own env → gets a 'procedure-tagged list → calls `apply-user-proc`.\n\nWhen my evaluator evaluates `(+ 1 2)`:\n1. Evaluates `+` in its own env → gets... what?\n\nThis is the key question. How do primitives get into my evaluator's environment?\n\nI need to create a global environment for my evaluator that includes all the primitives. There are two approaches:\n\n**Approach 1: Inherit from host environment**\nMake my evaluator's initial environment's parent point to the host environment. But the host environment contains eval.scm's own function definitions too, which might shadow things.\n\n**Approach 2: Copy primitives into a new environment**\nExplicitly define all primitives in a new environment.\n\nLet me go with Approach 1: create an environment whose parent is the host environment.\n\n```scheme\n(define (make-global-env)\n  (cons '() \n    (cons (list-of-builtins) \n      (cons (list-of-eval-scm-defs) '()))))\n```\n\nHmm, this is messy. Let me think more simply.\n\nIn Scheme (interp.py), the environment has `parent` pointers. When I create `(cons '() host-env)`, the new env's frame is empty and its parent is `host-env`. Lookups will first check the new frame, then the host env.\n\nThe host env contains:\n- All built-in primitives (+, -, *, /, car, cdr, cons, etc.)\n- All eval.scm's function definitions (eval-expr, env-define, assoc, etc.)\n\nIf I make my evaluator's env inherit from host env:\n- `+` → found in host env → Python lambda ✓\n- `eval-expr` → found in host env → Procedure ✓ (but wait, this is MY eval-expr! The inner evaluator shouldn't see the outer eval-expr...)\n\nWait, actually, for self-evaluation, when outer eval.scm interprets inner eval.scm:\n1. Outer eval.scm creates a new env for the inner program\n2. The inner program is eval.scm itself\n3. eval.scm's toplevel defines include `(define (eval-expr expr env) ...)`\n4. When the inner code evaluates `(eval-expr ...)`, it looks up in its env → finds... eval-expr\n\nIf the inner env inherits from host env, then `eval-expr` might resolve to the outer eval-expr (from host env). But that's wrong! The inner code should use its OWN `eval-expr`.\n\nSo I need to make sure the inner env has its own binding for `eval-expr` before the inner code runs.\n\nBut that's the problem with inheritance. When I evaluate `(define (eval-expr expr env) ...)` in the inner program, it adds a binding in the inner env's frame. After that, `eval-expr` resolves to the inner one.\n\nBut during the evaluation of `(define (eval-expr expr env) ...)`, when the body of eval-expr (in the inner program) calls `(eval-expr ...)`, which one does it use? The body of the inner eval-expr is evaluated when the procedure is APPLIED, not when it's defined. The procedure captures the env at definition time.\n\nWait, when interp.py evaluates `(define (eval-expr expr env) ...)` in the inner program:\n1. At definition time, `eval-expr` was not yet defined (we're in the process of defining it). So the closure env wouldn't have `eval-expr` in it. This is a bug for self-interpretation.\n\nTo fix this, I need `eval-expr` to be visible in the body before it's applied. In the host language, `define` is handled differently. Looking at interp.py's `eval_expr` for `define`:\n\n```python\nelif car == \"define\":\n    name_or_list = expr.cdr.car\n    if isinstance(name_or_list, Pair):\n        # Function definition shorthand\n        name = name_or_list.car\n        ...\n        proc = Procedure(params, [], env)\n        proc.body = ...\n        env.define(name, proc)\n```\n\nSo `env.define(name, proc)` is called with the SAME `env` (the current environment). And `Procedure(params, [], env)` captures the current env (before `define` has added the binding). Let me check.\n\nWhen interp.py evaluates this define:\n1. `env.define('factorial', proc)` adds `factorial` to the current env\n2. `proc` captures the current env (BEFORE the define call, or at the time of `Procedure(params, [], env)` creation)\n\nWait, let me re-read the code:\n```python\nproc = Procedure(params, [], env)  # env at this point doesn't have 'factorial' yet\nproc.body = ...\nenv.define(name, proc)  # Now 'factorial' is in env\n```\n\nSo the closure env doesn't have `factorial` in it. When I later modify that Environment object (via `env.define`), the Procedure sees the modification because it's the same object.\n\nNow, in my metacircular evaluator, how do I handle this?\n\nI'm representing environments as pairs of `(frame . parent)`. When I do `(env-define env name value)`, I modify the frame by doing `(set-car! env (cons (cons name value) (car env)))`. This modifies the actual cons cell that the environment references.\n\nBut wait - in my evaluator, when I create a procedure:\n```scheme\n(env-define env name\n  (cons 'procedure\n    (cons params\n      (cons body env))))\n```\n\nHere, `env` is captured in the procedure's closure. After `(env-define env name ...)`, the `env` pair's car (the frame) is modified to include the new binding. Let me continue.\n\n### eval-if\n\n```scheme\n(define (eval-if expr env)\n  (let ((condition (eval-expr (cadr expr) env)))\n    (if condition\n        (eval-expr (caddr expr) env)\n        (if (not (null? When my evaluator is running, every `(eval-expr ...)` call goes through interp.py's `eval_expr`. So the depth increases with each recursive call in my evaluator.\n\nFor a simple program like calculator.scm, the depth is shallow. For a recursive function like factorial(20), the depth would be ~20 (one call per recursive step in the interpreted program + some overhead for the evaluator).\n\nFor self-evaluation with a recursive function, we need depth for each layer:\n- Layer 1 (outer eval.scm): evaluating the inner eval.scm's eval-expr\n- Layer 2 (inner eval.scm): evaluating the inner eval-expr which processes factorial\n- Layer 3: factorial recursion\n\nEach factorial recursive call needs:\n- Layer 2 evaluates the inner eval-expr body\n- Which evaluates factorial(5) → evaluates (factorial (- 5 1)) → ...\n- Each recursive call goes through layer 2\n\nThe depth would be roughly (factorial depth) * (overhead per recursive call). The depth per recursive call is roughly the depth of eval-expr's own internal calls, which might be 10-20 levels of host eval_expr.\n\nFor factorial(20): 20 * 15 = 300 levels. Still well under 5000/10000.\n\nFor self-evaluation of a deeply recursive program... factorial(100) might hit 1500 levels. Still OK.\n\nWhat about deeply nested expressions? `(+ (+ (+ (+ 1 2) 3) 4) 5)` has a parse tree depth of 5. My eval-expr would handle it with depth proportional to the tree depth.
